**Plausible architectural explanation, flagged as a hypothesis, not confirmed mechanistically:** a GAN's generator is trained under direct adversarial pressure from a critic that has specifically learned to recognize implausible (fake-looking) outputs -- a built-in force discouraging wildly-off-manifold generations under any conditioning, including an unfamiliar pushed target. Diffusion's denoising-score-matching objective and cVAE's KL-toward-a-smooth-Gaussian-prior objective both lack that specific "does this look real" pressure; cVAE's own smoothing (§18-20's whole "cVAE is the blurriest, least confident architecture" pattern, its comparatively weak steering signal here too) may substitute a different kind of implicit regularization (regression toward the training mean under uncertain conditioning) that happens to keep it safe here, while diffusion's iterative denoising process has no equivalent found in this project's evidence to point to and, per this result, extrapolates far more boldly (and precisely, when it lands validly) but with no guardrail against overshooting into physically impossible territory. This double-explains both halves of §37's original finding: diffusion's convergent candidates are the MOST accurately steered because it commits fully to the requested direction rather than blending conservatively, and its convergence is the WORST for exactly the same reason -- committing fully sometimes means committing to a nonphysical design.

**Practical implication**: none of this project's 3 generative architectures fail uniformly across domains and settings, another instance of the "no single best architecture" pattern from §34/§37 -- but for TORAX specifically, diffusion's failure mode is now understood well enough that a targeted mitigation is plausible (e.g. clipping/rejecting decoded params against the input-side sanity caps before even calling the oracle, cheap since it's a pure post-decode check) -- not built here, flagged as a natural next step.

**Open:**
- The adversarial-pressure explanation is plausible, not tested -- would need e.g. a controlled ablation (a diffusion variant with an added realism/regularization term) to actually confirm, not attempted this pass.
- Diagnostic run once, seed=0, step_std=1.0 for the mechanistic breakdown (Step 3) -- the magnitude-sweep numbers (Step 1) are the only part of this section run across multiple step_std values.
- Not checked whether this same catastrophic-extrapolation mechanism shows up for diffusion in VMEC++/airfoil too, retroactively -- both of those domains' diffusion models have never had their failing candidates' raw params inspected this way; this diagnostic script is TORAX-specific (imports `train_torax_cvae.PARAM_NAMES`/`_PARAM_CAPS` directly) rather than generalized across domains.
- No mitigation (param-space rejection/clipping) actually implemented or measured yet.

## 39. A second interactive viewer: the stellarator design-browser generalizes, its physics panel doesn't -- built airfoils' own from real XFOIL data

Direct user request: does Vega's stellarator viewer (`viewer/`, ~3,800 lines across `index.astro`/`lib/*.ts`/`physics_service.py`, built over many prior sessions) generalize to airfoils/TORAX? Read the existing viewer fully before answering rather than guessing from its file names. Honest split found: the LEFT-panel design-browser (UMAP embedding over target metrics, per-target histogram range filters, candidate browser) reads a domain-agnostic `meta.json`/`X.bin`/`Y.bin` contract with a `feature_layout` name->offset map -- genuinely reusable, needs only a per-domain export script. The RIGHT-panel physics view does NOT generalize: the Three.js geometry hardcodes VMEC's 90-coefficient R-Z Fourier synthesis, and `physics_service.py` calls VMEC++/DESC equilibrium solvers specifically -- neither concept exists for airfoils (2D aerodynamics, not 3D magnetic equilibrium) or TORAX (1.5D radial transport on a fixed simple geometry, no field-line/Boozer-coordinate machinery at all in this project's build). User chose to start with airfoils (simpler, no new background-job service needed) and go functional-first over matching the stellarator viewer's full visual polish immediately.

**Confirmed each domain's own real "shape + physics field" analog before building anything**, mirroring the stellarator's "3D surface + |B|" pairing: airfoils have XFOIL's real `get_cp_distribution()` (confirmed directly -- a genuine pressure-coefficient curve, not something to fabricate); TORAX has real per-radius, per-timestep profiles (`T_e`, `T_i`, `n_e`, `q_face`, current density, confirmed across 22 timesteps on a 25-point grid) -- flagged for a later TORAX pass, not built this section.

**Built for airfoils, end-to-end, confirmed working via a live browser check (screenshots + console), not just "the code compiles":**

- `scripts/export_airfoil_viewer_data.py` -- produces `viewer/public/data/airfoil/{meta.json,X.bin,Y.bin}` in the exact same format as the stellarator data, with TWO sources: `bootstrap` (2,500-row random subsample of §22's generated dataset, same sanity filter every other script applies) and `real_naca` (all 33 rows of §36's real NACA 0012/2412/4415 x 11-alpha polars) -- the design-browser gets a real-vs-synthetic comparison the stellarator viewer's own data never had.
- `scripts/airfoil_physics_service.py` (new FastAPI service, `physics-airfoil` in docker-compose, port 8091, built on `Dockerfile.train` since that's the only image with XFOIL) -- one synchronous POST /physics endpoint (no job-polling machinery needed, unlike `physics_service.py`'s VMEC++/DESC case: XFOIL converges in ~15-45ms, confirmed §21) returning the candidate's own (x,y) shape coordinates plus a real `get_cp_distribution()` call's pressure curve and cl/cd/cm. Needed `fastapi`/`uvicorn` added to `Dockerfile.train` (confirmed it doesn't reopen the numpy<2 conflict -- that pin is specific to this image's older torch 2.2.0 build, not a general torch/fastapi incompatibility).
- `viewer/src/pages/airfoil.astro` (new page, not a modification of the stellarator one) -- reuses `colormap.ts`/`math.ts`'s `tex()` helper directly (both already domain-agnostic), ports the filter/histogram/UMAP interaction code from `index.astro` (same drag-to-set-range, click-to-edit, UMAP-recompute mechanics, adapted for 4 targets instead of 11-12), and replaces the VMEC-specific 3D scene with a plain 2D canvas airfoil-profile renderer (client-side CST/Bernstein decode, mirroring `airfoil_oracle.cst_to_coords` in JS so the browser doesn't need a round-trip just to draw the shape) plus a live Cp-distribution canvas plot fed by the new physics service.

**Verified live in a real browser** (`claude-in-chrome`, not just curl): loads with zero console errors; UMAP renders 800 sampled points with `real_naca` rows visibly distinct (larger, outlined markers); clicking a UMAP point / filtering by source / paging with prev-next-random all correctly update the shape+Cp panels through a real oracle call each time; selecting a `real_naca` row (global row 2,500, alpha=-4°) reproduced `cl=-0.40434`, matching §36's independently-computed NACA 0012 value (`-0.4043`) to 4 decimals -- the viewer's client-side CST decode and the Python-side `cst_to_coords` agree, not assumed.

**Open:**
- TORAX's own physics panel (radial profile evolution) not built this pass -- needs a caching background-job service like `physics_service.py`'s (TORAX calls are not XFOIL-fast) rather than the airfoil page's simple synchronous pattern, and a genuinely different visualization (time-series line charts, not a static shape).
- Visual polish deliberately deferred (functional-first, per direct user choice) -- no KaTeX-labeled axes, no hover tooltips on the Cp plot, source-card highlight-on-hover works but isn't visually refined; the stellarator viewer's own polish took many sessions, not assumed achievable in one pass here.
- The metric-header expand/collapse chevron (ported from `index.astro`) wasn't confirmed working in the live check -- everything load-bearing (filtering, browsing, physics) was verified; this cosmetic detail was not.
- `export_airfoil_viewer_data.py`'s 2,500-row bootstrap subsample is arbitrary (browser-payload-size driven), not chosen for any particular coverage property.

## 40. TORAX's own interactive viewer, plus cross-viewer navigation -- the radial-profile-evolution visualization flagged as future work in §39, built and confirmed against the real reference numbers

Direct follow-up: added a small `.domain-nav` link row (Stellarators / Airfoils / TORAX) to every viewer page's header so switching domains doesn't require typing a URL by hand, then built TORAX's own physics panel -- the piece §39 explicitly deferred, since it needed a genuinely different visualization (radial profile evolution, not a static shape) and a different backend pattern (TORAX calls aren't XFOIL-fast).

**`scripts/export_torax_viewer_data.py`**: same `meta.json`/`X.bin`/`Y.bin` contract as the airfoil export, two sources -- `bootstrap` (2,500-row subsample of §35's dataset, filtered by BOTH the target-side and input-side sanity caps from §35/§37, not a new filter invented here) and `real_devices` (all 4 rows from §36's `real_reference_torax.py` -- ITER baseline, ITER hybrid flattop, SPARC, JET). Only 4 real rows (this domain's real-reference set is named devices, not a swept condition like airfoils' 11 angles per shape), but genuinely real.

**`scripts/torax_physics_service.py`** (new, `physics-torax` service, port 8092, on `Dockerfile.torax` -- needed `fastapi`/`uvicorn` added there): unlike the airfoil service's simple stateless-per-request pattern, this warms the JIT cache with one throwaway `run_simulation` call at server startup (`@app.on_event("startup")`) -- confirmed necessary (§25/§31/§37's ~4.9s cold-compile tax), so the service stays synchronous like airfoil's rather than needing `physics_service.py`'s full job-polling protocol (that exists for VMEC++/DESC because a solve itself can take seconds on EVERY call, not just the first -- a genuinely different cost shape). Runs a full simulation (not just the 4 final scalars `torax_oracle.py`'s `worker_fn` returns) and extracts the complete radial-profile time history -- `T_e`/`T_i`/`n_e`/`j_total` on the 25 cell centers, `q_face` on the 26 face points, across all ~22 timesteps -- confirmed available by direct inspection before writing any code (not assumed), reusing `torax_oracle._build_config` rather than duplicating the config-building logic.

**`viewer/src/pages/torax.astro`** (new page): same UMAP/filter/candidate-browser pattern as the other two pages, center panel replaced with (1) a poloidal cross-section -- a plain ellipse from the candidate's own `R_major`/`a_minor`/`elongation_LCFS`, the natural TORAX analogue of the stellarator's 3D surface and the airfoil's 2D profile, genuinely simple since this project's TORAX parameterization is a fixed circular/elongated geometry, not a free-boundary shape -- and (2) four small radial-profile line charts (T_e/T_i, n_e, q, j_total) driven by a time slider plus a play/pause button that steps through the real simulation history.

**Verified live in a real browser, not just curl**: zero console errors; selecting the `real_devices` source and its first row (global row 2,500, R_major=6.20m/B_0=5.30T/Ip=15.00MA -- ITER baseline's real parameters) reproduced **Q_fusion=3.16, tau_E=1.3779, H98=0.66185, T_e_volume_avg=7.2073** -- an exact match to §36's independently-computed reference values, confirming the viewer's full path (candidate row -> `/physics` POST -> real `run_simulation` -> rendered profile) is correct end to end, not just that it renders *something*. The rendered profiles are textbook-shaped too: T_e/T_i falling from a hot core to a cool edge, q rising outward from ~1.2 (core) past 1 as expected for a well-confined discharge, j_total peaked off-axis -- consistent with the raw numbers checked directly while building the backend.

**Open:**
- Play/pause button interaction wasn't confirmed working in the live check (time-slider dragging and the initial final-frame render were); everything load-bearing (candidate browsing, the physics call, profile rendering, the reference-value match) was verified.
- Visual polish deliberately still minimal, matching §39's functional-first choice -- no axis labels/units overlay on the profile charts beyond the panel captions, no hover readout on the profile lines.
- `physics-torax`'s JIT warmup happens once per container start; a container restart (e.g. during `--reload` after an edit) pays the ~4.9s tax again on the next request -- acceptable for a dev service, not addressed for anything more permanent.
- No coverage-metric or steerability-anchor integration with the viewer for any of the three domains yet -- these viewers are browsers/inspectors, not yet wired into `bootstrap_generic.py`/`steerability_generic.py`'s own anchor selection.

## 41. A real 3D tokamak for the TORAX viewer -- nested flux surfaces colored by live T_e, replacing the flat 2D cross-section

Direct follow-up: user asked for an actual 3D visualization, not the 2D poloidal-cross-section canvas §40 shipped with. This project's TORAX parameterization has no free-boundary shape the way VMEC's stellarator boundary does -- it's `torax_oracle.py`'s own fixed `geometry_type: "circular"` (§31), so there's no equivalent of `index.astro`'s Fourier-coefficient surface synthesis to port. What TORAX genuinely has instead is real *radial* structure (§40's profile history) -- the natural 3D idea is therefore nested flux surfaces (the standard tokamak-cutaway convention), not a single fancier boundary shape.

**Built directly into `torax.astro`** (Three.js + `OrbitControls`, already project dependencies, reused rather than re-added): flux surfaces are modeled as concentric, self-similar ellipses shrinking linearly with rho (`R_major + rho*a_minor*cos(theta)`, `rho*a_minor*elongation_LCFS*sin(theta)`, revolved through a `phi` range of ~297 degrees) -- a real simplification stated plainly in the code comment (this models TORAX's own circular-geometry ansatz, not a claim about true shaped-plasma flux surfaces), not smuggled in as if it were exact. 9 shells (every 3rd of the 25 `rho_cell_norm` points, plus the LCFS) are built once per candidate (`buildTokamakShells`), each an unlit semi-transparent `MeshBasicMaterial` mesh colored by that shell's ACTUAL `T_e` value at the current timestep (`updateShellColors`, plasma colormap, normalized against the candidate's own full time-history min/max) -- the wedge left open (not a full 360 degrees) so the hot inner shells are visible through the cooler outer ones, the standard cutaway-diagram convention, not an arbitrary stylistic choice. The LCFS shell additionally gets a wireframe overlay for a crisp boundary edge.

**Wired to the existing time slider, not a separate control**: `renderProfilesAtFrame(k)` (already driving the 4 2D radial-profile charts, §40) now also calls `updateShellColors(profiles, k)` -- scrubbing or playing through the simulation timeline reheats/cools the 3D shells in sync with the 2D charts, both views of the same real data rather than two disconnected widgets.

**Verified live in a real browser**: zero console errors; orbit-drag rotation works (confirmed the wedge cut is genuinely open, not just a flat texture, by rotating to see through it); dragging the time slider to t=0 visibly changed both the 3D shell colors and the 2D T_e/T_i curves together, confirming the shared-state wiring is real, not coincidental.

**Open:**
- The concentric-self-similar-ellipse flux-surface model is a stated simplification (see above) -- fine for this project's own circular-geometry TORAX config, would NOT be appropriate to reuse for a real shaped-plasma equilibrium without revisiting.
- No lighting/shading depth cues beyond flat unlit color + partial transparency -- readable, not polished; `depthWrite: false` on the transparent shells avoids the worst sorting artifacts but isn't a full order-independent-transparency solution, not chased further since the result already reads clearly in practice.
- Shell count (9) and phi-wedge span (~297 degrees) are chosen by eye for a readable result, not derived from any principled criterion.

## 42. Plain-language metric tooltips across all three viewers, grounded in the real problem definitions and this project's own reference numbers, not textbook recall

Direct user request: explain in plain terms what each target metric means, what reasonable/reference values look like, and why it's tracked at all -- across all 20 metrics shown across the three viewers (12 stellarator, 4 airfoil, 4 TORAX). Read the authoritative sources directly rather than writing from memory, matching this project's own standing discipline: for the 12 stellarator metrics, that meant inspecting `constellaration.problems`' own docstrings (`GeometricalProblem`, `SimpleToBuildQIStellarator`, `MHDStableQIStellarator` -- Proxima Fusion's own three published ConStellaration benchmark problems, confirmed by reading their source in the `train` image, not assumed) for both the "why it matters" language and each metric's REAL numeric constraint bound (e.g. `aspect_ratio<=4.0`/`<=10.0` depending on the problem, `edge_magnetic_mirror_ratio<=0.2-0.25`, `log10(qi)<=-3.5 to -4`, `vacuum_well>=0`, `max_elongation` scored 1=circular/best to 10=worst) -- these are the actual bounds Proxima Fusion's own optimization problems use, not invented rules of thumb. For airfoil/TORAX, used this project's own already-computed real numbers instead of textbook lookups: airfoil references cite §36's real NACA 0012/2412/4415 polars directly (e.g. cd's reference line quotes the real 0.00513-at-alpha=0-rising-to-0.011-by-alpha=10 shape from that section); TORAX references cite §36/§40's real ITER-baseline/SPARC-geometry runs (Q_fusion=3.16/0.19, tau_E=1.38s, H98=0.66) alongside well-known public facts (ITER's real Q~10 target, JET's real 1997 Q~0.67 record) with an explicit caveat that this project's own device runs use generic (non-tuned) transport settings, not each real device's own predicted performance -- not overclaiming equivalence.

**Built `viewer/src/lib/metricInfo.ts`** (new, shared across all three pages since none of the 20 metric names collide across domains) -- one `METRIC_INFO` lookup of `{blurb, reference}` per metric. Wired into each page's existing `buildFilterRows()` (all three already had an expand/collapse metric-card per target, from the original stellarator viewer's own design) by inserting an `.explain-box` div at the top of the expanded card body, above the histogram -- reusing `index.astro`'s own `.explain-box` style (already used for that page's physics-mode explanation text) rather than inventing new chrome; added the same CSS to `airfoil.astro`/`torax.astro`, which didn't have it (airfoil.astro had an unused simplified version already sitting in its stylesheet from the Cp-plot caption -- upgraded in place rather than left as dead style).

**Verified live in all three viewers, not just written and assumed correct**: zero console errors on any page; expanding a metric card renders the explanation text with all special characters intact (checked directly via zoomed screenshots) -- subscripts (log₁₀), inequality signs (≤), the rotational-transform symbol (ι), arrows (→) in the Q_fusion blurb, and em-dashes, none garbled.

**Open:**
- No hover-tooltip variant (mouseover without clicking) -- the explanation lives in the same expand/collapse interaction the histogram already uses, consistent with the existing UI pattern but requires a click, not a hover, to see.
- The airfoil/TORAX reference lines lean on this project's OWN prior real-data computations (§36/§40) rather than independent textbook citations for every claim -- appropriate given those are real, verified numbers this project produced directly, but worth remembering they're not a substitute for, say, an XFOIL/TORAX textbook's own stated "typical" ranges if a reader wanted that instead.
- Not reviewed by a domain expert for correctness beyond this project's own source-checking discipline (reading `constellaration.problems`' real docstrings, quoting this project's own verified reference runs) -- confident in the sourcing, not claiming external peer review.

## 43. Restructured §42's tooltips into hover-bubble + dedicated glossary pages, and a real CSS clipping bug found and fixed along the way

Direct user feedback on §42: the inline expand-to-read paragraph was too much text living inside the filter panel. Asked for the "classic i in a bubble" pattern (one short sentence, on hover) plus a separate dedicated glossary page per domain for the full explanation.

**`metricInfo.ts` extended, not replaced**: added a `short` field (one plain sentence) to all 20 entries alongside the existing `blurb`/`reference`, plus a new `DOMAIN_METRICS` map (`{stellarator: [...12], airfoil: [...4], torax: [...4]}`) so the glossary pages can iterate a domain's metrics without re-listing names by hand.

**Three new static glossary pages** (`viewer/src/pages/glossary/{stellarator,airfoil,torax}.astro`) -- pure server-rendered Astro (no client JS needed, content computed at build time from `METRIC_INFO`/`DOMAIN_METRICS`), one card per metric with the full blurb + reference, cross-linked to each other and back to their own viewer. Each viewer's Filters card header gained a "metric glossary ↗" link (opens in a new tab) next to "reset all".

**Removed the inline `.explain-box` from every metric-card body**, replaced with a small "i" bubble (`.info-icon`, a 15px circle) directly in the collapsed metric-header, next to the metric name -- visible without expanding the row at all, matching the request directly.

**A real bug found while wiring the hover, not assumed to just work:** the first attempt used a pure-CSS `:hover::after` tooltip (content from a `data-tip` attribute, no JS). It never appeared. Traced directly (not guessed) via `getBoundingClientRect()` in the browser console: `.metric-card` has its own `overflow:hidden` (there to keep the collapsed row's corners clean against the card's border-radius, confirmed by reading that rule), which clips ANY absolutely-positioned descendant that escapes the row's own collapsed bounds -- true in both directions (`bottom:130%` tried first, then `top:130%`, both clipped identically), so this wasn't a positioning-direction bug, it was a fundamental ancestor-overflow conflict pure CSS couldn't route around without either removing that overflow rule (risking a different cosmetic regression on the card's corners) or fighting it. **Fixed with a small amount of JS instead of removing the overflow rule**: one shared `#metricTooltip` element per page, appended outside the clipping ancestor (`position: fixed`, so it's positioned relative to the viewport, immune to any ancestor's `overflow`), a delegated `mouseover`/`mouseout` listener on the filters container reads `.info-icon`'s `data-tip` and the hovered icon's own `getBoundingClientRect()` to position it. More robust than the pure-CSS attempt, not just a workaround -- doesn't depend on any particular DOM nesting staying overflow-safe in the future.

**Verified live across all three viewers** (`claude-in-chrome` + direct DOM queries via the JS execution tool, not just a glance): confirmed 12/4/4 `.info-icon` elements exist per page with correct `data-tip` text matching `metricInfo.ts` exactly; confirmed the first (pure-CSS) attempt visibly failed to show anything on hover; confirmed the fixed (JS/fixed-position) version renders the correct one-sentence tooltip directly below the hovered icon on all three pages; zero console errors throughout. All three glossary pages load cleanly and render every metric's full text correctly.

**Open:**
- The `.metric-tooltip` positioning math (`rect.left - 100`, clamped to the viewport) is a simple centered-ish heuristic, not pixel-perfect edge-avoidance for every possible row width/icon position -- fine in practice for this layout, not a general-purpose tooltip library.
- No touch/mobile equivalent (hover has no direct touch analogue) -- the glossary page is the fallback for that case, reachable via the link, not designed around it specifically.
- The three glossary pages don't share a single Astro layout/component (each repeats the same ~40 lines of page chrome) -- consistent with this project's established "duplicate small page-scoped chunks rather than build a shared framework" convention elsewhere in `viewer/`, not an oversight.

## 44. A fourth domain, chosen and spiked: JAX-Fluids ruled out on license, jax-cfd picked instead and given TORAX's own §25 treatment

Direct user request for a fourth Optimization Gym domain, GPU-based specifically. Re-read §27's `CANDIDATE_TRIAGE`: three candidates were flagged "needs batched worker" on the JIT-tax hypothesis TORAX already confirmed (Warp FEM, JAX-Fluids, Warp Darcy) -- JAX-Fluids picked first as the closest infra match, since it's JAX-native like TORAX and should be able to reuse the exact `jax.vmap`/`persistent_worker_fn` split already built and proven in §31/§37, unlike the two Warp candidates which compile via CUDA graphs, a genuinely different batching model.

**Real blocker found before writing any integration code: JAX-Fluids is GPL-3.0-only**, confirmed by fetching `github.com/tumaer/JAXFLUIDS`'s actual `LICENSE` file directly (not inferred from the README) -- same discipline §25 applied to TORAX's license. Every domain in this project so far (VMEC++, XFOIL/NeuralFoil, TORAX) was confirmed permissively licensed (Apache/MIT/BSD-family) before any code was written against it; GPL-3.0's copyleft would put obligations on the combined codebase, not just an isolated module, the way this project imports oracle modules directly into `gym_schema.py`. Also not on PyPI at all (confirmed via the PyPI JSON API returning 404) -- git-clone-and-`pip install .` only, the same install friction §21/§25 already flagged once for XFOIL. Flagged to the user directly rather than deciding unilaterally; user chose to switch domains rather than accept the licensing exposure.

**Switched to `jax-cfd`** (google/jax-cfd) -- Apache 2.0 (confirmed via its own `LICENSE` file), pip-installable (real wheel on PyPI, `jax-cfd==0.2.1`), JAX-native. Narrower in scope than JAX-Fluids -- incompressible Navier-Stokes (finite-difference/finite-volume, `jax_cfd.base`) rather than compressible two-phase flow -- but same GPU/harness-fit profile as TORAX and no license risk.

**Given TORAX's exact §25 spike treatment: installed for real, ran for real, read the source for the failure contract, not the README.**

- **A real compatibility break found immediately, not assumed away**: `jax-cfd==0.2.1` (its only PyPI release; confirmed via `pip index versions`) calls `jax.tree_map`, removed in JAX 0.6.0 -- `pip install "jax[cuda12]" jax-cfd` (latest jax, 0.6.2) fails on the very first simulation step with `AttributeError: jax.tree_map was removed in JAX v0.6.0`. This is a genuine unmaintained-package risk this project hasn't hit with any prior domain (VMEC++/XFOIL/TORAX are all either actively maintained or simple enough to not touch deprecated JAX/numpy internals) -- **worked around, not fixed at the source**, by pinning `jax[cuda12]==0.4.34` (last release still exposing `jax.tree_map`); GPU (`CudaDevice`) still detected fine at that pin. A real Dockerfile for this domain would need to carry that same pin, isolated the same way `Dockerfile.torax` isolates its own numpy>=2 requirement from the rest of the project's images.

- **JIT-tax and vmap-batching hypothesis confirmed, real numbers, same shape as TORAX's**: a 64x64 2D Kolmogorov-forced-turbulence step function (`jax_cfd.base.equations.semi_implicit_navier_stokes`), cold call (fresh trace + compile) 1.78s, warm re-call 0.25s (JAX's own compilation cache, not explicit `jax.jit` on my part). Explicitly `jax.jit`-wrapped and called three times with three different viscosities (same array shapes each time): 1st call 0.50s (compile), 2nd/3rd calls 0.001-0.002s -- confirms scalar physics parameters don't trigger recompilation under `jax.jit`, only shape/dtype changes do, exactly the property TORAX's `batch_worker_fn` already depends on. `jax.vmap` batch test across viscosities, batch sizes 1/8/32/128: compile cost stays flat (~0.58-0.65s) regardless of batch size, warm per-candidate cost drops 1.40ms -> 0.03ms as batch grows -- GPU not saturated even at 128, the same "batching is essentially free until you hit device limits" finding §31's TORAX spike made. **Reads as: TORAX's already-built `persistent_worker_fn`/`batch_worker_fn` split should transfer to this domain directly, not need new infra design** -- the one piece of TORAX's own harness-fit cost that *doesn't* need re-paying here.

- **Failure signaling is a real step down from every existing domain, not just "not as good as TORAX."** VMEC++ has a converged flag, XFOIL has `worker_fn`'s cd-based rejection, TORAX has a 7-value `SimError` enum (§25) -- `jax_cfd` has **none**: deliberately drove a config unstable (viscosity 1e-5, dt=5.0, a real CFL violation for that grid) and it "ran to completion" with a silent all-NaN state, no exception, no flag, nothing in the return value distinguishing it from a converged run. Building this domain would mean adding NaN/inf detection ourselves as the entire validity signal (`ok = not jnp.isnan(result).any()`, roughly) -- cheap to add, but a real gap worth naming plainly rather than assuming jax_cfd's polish matches TORAX's.

- **No obstacle/immersed-body primitive -- a real, unresolved design-parameterization question, unlike every other domain here.** Read `jax_cfd.base.boundaries` directly: only `periodic`, `dirichlet`, `neumann`, and `channel_flow` boundary conditions exist, no masked/immersed-obstacle mechanism the way an airfoil-in-flow or cylinder-drag CFD benchmark would need. VMEC++'s x is a boundary Fourier spectrum, XFOIL's is an airfoil's CST coefficients, TORAX's is a transport config -- each domain's "design" was obvious from what the oracle already parameterizes. jax_cfd has no equivalently obvious design vector: the most direct path with what's actually in the package is parameterizing the **channel-flow wall geometry** (`channel_flow_boundary_conditions`) or a **body-force/porous-media field** layered on top of the existing forcing mechanism, neither confirmed to work, both requiring real implementation before there's an `x -> oracle(x) -> y` shape to wrap in `DomainSpec` at all. This is the one open item TORAX's own §25 spike didn't have (TORAX's x -- transport config parameters -- was unambiguous from the start).

**Not done this pass, staying honest about scope**: no `DomainSpec`/`gym_schema.py` entry, no Dockerfile, no dataset, no oracle module, no design-parameterization decision made -- this is the oracle-level feasibility spike only, the same stage TORAX was at after §25 before §31 built on top of it. Packages installed to the host's user site-packages for this spike (`~/.local/lib/python3.10/site-packages`), not inside a project Docker image -- fine for a spike, would need a real `Dockerfile.jaxcfd` (Python 3.10+, `jax[cuda12]==0.4.34` pin, `jax-cfd`) before anything durable is built.

**Open:**
- The design-parameterization question above is the real next decision -- channel-wall-shape vs. body-force-field vs. something else -- before any oracle module, dataset, or Dockerfile makes sense.
- NaN/inf-based validity checking is proposed, not implemented or tested against a real batch of adversarial configs the way §21 did for airfoils.
- The `jax==0.4.34` pin is a real ceiling on jax-cfd's own bit-rot -- worth periodically checking upstream (github.com/google/jax-cfd) for a `jax.tree_util.tree_map`-updated fork or a maintained successor before committing to this pin long-term.
- Only 2D periodic/Kolmogorov-forced turbulence was spiked -- no 3D, no non-periodic domain, no confirmation that `channel_flow_boundary_conditions` actually works end-to-end (only imported/listed, not run).

## 45. jax-cfd's design parameterization, resolved for real: wall BCs are unimplemented, a fully-periodic penalized nozzle works and passes its physical-sanity check

Direct follow-up to §44's open item, and a real user question first ("does jax_cfd want a mesh instead of a parameterization?") that surfaced the right framing: jax_cfd has no immersed-obstacle primitive at all, mesh or otherwise -- confirmed once more while chasing this section's own dead end below. User picked the nozzle/duct-wall-shape direction (§44's option 1) to spike for real.

**§44's `channel_flow_boundary_conditions` turned out to be unimplemented in the actual solver, not just untested -- confirmed three independent ways, not one guess:**
1. The default advection schemes (`advect_van_leer`/`advect_linear`/`advect_upwind`, all routed through `advect_general`) contain an explicit guard: `if not boundaries.has_all_periodic_boundary_conditions(c): raise NotImplementedError(...)`.
2. Swapping in `convect_linear` (a separate code path that doesn't call `advect_general`) instead crashed with an internal `ValueError: the GridArray does not contain all interior grid values` deep in `boundaries.py`'s own offset-alignment bookkeeping, using `HomogeneousBoundaryConditions`.
3. Retried with explicit `ConstantBoundaryConditions` (real Dirichlet zero values instead of the homogeneous default) instead of chasing (2) further -- hit a *different*, cleaner, explicit guard: `get_advection_flux_bc_from_velocity_and_scalar` itself raises `NotImplementedError('Flux boundary condition is not implemented for velocity with ConstantBoundaryConditions(...dirichlet...))`.

Three separate code paths, three separate confirmations: `jax_cfd.base`'s convection step -- the core PDE term, not an edge feature -- genuinely does not support wall-bounded (non-periodic) flow in the public 0.2.1 release, despite `boundaries.py` shipping a whole `channel_flow_boundary_conditions` constructor that implies otherwise. (Pressure projection, checked separately, is *not* the blocker -- `pressure.solve_fast_diag` already auto-switches to a matmul-based non-circulant solve for non-periodic BCs and has a dedicated `solve_fast_diag_channel_flow` variant; convection is the only broken piece.) Not chased into a jax_cfd source patch -- treated as a hard library limitation to design around, the same way §21 treated XFOIL's own missing OpenFOAM tier as a real gap rather than something to fix upstream.

**Worked around by never invoking the broken code path at all**, not by fixing it: fully **periodic** domain in both dimensions (the one BC type §44 already proved works end-to-end), with the actual duct -- both the constant-width outer pipe walls *and* the converging-diverging nozzle constriction -- represented entirely as a **Brinkman-penalization body force** (`-mask(x,y) * velocity / epsilon`, epsilon=2e-3) added to a constant streamwise driving force, rather than as any kind of geometric/BC boundary. `x` = 1-2 Fourier coefficients on `cos((k+1) * 2*pi*x/LX)`, added to a fixed base half-height -- inherently periodicity-consistent (no seam discontinuity at x=0/LX by construction, unlike a boundary-fitted mesh would need to enforce by hand).

**A real coordinate-order bug caught by the sanity check itself, not by inspection**: first run gave fluid_frac 0.12-0.21 (way too little open channel for a nominal half-height of 0.42, i.e. ~84% should be fluid) and a *non-monotonic* bulk-velocity response to constriction tightening -- traced to `grid.mesh()` returning `(x_mesh, y_mesh)` in that order, but the mask-building code had unpacked it as `ys, xs = grid.mesh(...)`, silently swapping which axis the constriction narrowed. Confirmed the correct order directly (`grid.mesh()[0].max() == LX`, `[1].max() == LY`) before fixing, the same "verify, don't assume" bar §21/§30 already held other axis/shape bugs to.

**After the fix, the physical-sanity check this project always runs before trusting a new oracle shape (§9/§21's own convention) passes cleanly**: bulk mean streamwise velocity for a fixed driving-force magnitude, three constriction levels --

| constriction | fluid area fraction | bulk mean u | NaN? |
|---|--:|--:|--:|
| none | 0.844 | 2.934 | no |
| mild | 0.825 | 2.750 | no |
| tight | 0.739 | 1.277 | no |

Monotonically decreasing (confirmed programmatically, not eyeballed) -- a narrower throat produces measurably more resistance and less throughput for the same driving force, exactly the physical direction a real constriction should move it, and a large, clearly-outside-noise effect (2.9 -> 1.3, more than 2x) rather than a marginal one. `fluid_frac` for the unconstricted case (0.844) also lands almost exactly on the analytically-expected value (2 x 0.42 / 1.0 = 0.84) -- a second, independent confirmation the geometry is being built correctly, not just that the physics trend happens to point the right way. Fast: ~0.24s warm per candidate at 128x64 on GPU (first call ~0.93s, compile tax, consistent with §44's own vmap-batching numbers).

**Reads as: a real, working `x -> oracle -> y` shape now exists for this domain** -- `x` = Fourier coefficients on the constriction profile, `y` = bulk throughput (and, not yet extracted, throat velocity / pressure drop / separation indicators from the same run) -- closing §44's one open item that every other domain here didn't have to solve. Still a spike, not a build: single grid resolution (128x64), single viscosity/forcing magnitude, no convergence/resolution check, no validity signal beyond NaN-checking (§44's already-flagged gap), no `DomainSpec`, no Dockerfile, no dataset.

**Open:**
- No `DomainSpec`/`gym_schema.py` entry, oracle module, Dockerfile, or dataset built yet -- same "feasibility confirmed, not yet productionized" stage as TORAX after §25, now with the design-parameterization question also closed (TORAX never had this problem to begin with).
- Only bulk throughput was extracted as `y` -- richer targets (throat velocity, pressure drop across the constriction, a recirculation/separation-zone size behind the throat) are physically available from the same run but not yet computed.
- Penalization strength (`epsilon=2e-3`) and grid resolution (128x64) are both unvalidated choices -- no check yet that results are epsilon-converged (penalization should approach a true no-slip wall as epsilon->0, not confirmed) or grid-converged (no resolution sweep).
- Only up to 2 Fourier harmonics tried -- unconfirmed how many are needed for a rich-enough design space, or whether higher harmonics produce unphysical (self-intersecting/negative-half-height) geometries that would need a validity check of their own, the kind VMEC++ and airfoils both needed (§9, §22).
- The `jax.tree_map`/`jax==0.4.34` pin from §44 still applies here -- this section's spike ran under the same pin, not re-verified against a fixed-upstream jax-cfd.

## 46. A 5th domain, chosen for the opposite reason §44 picked jax-cfd: deliberately CPU-only, self-written, intuitive -- insulated-mug thermal design spiked and passing its own sanity checks

User pushed back directly on the premise behind §44's domain search: picking a domain *because* it's GPU-native/JAX-batchable selects specifically against the case this project's whole surrogate/generation toolkit is built for (an oracle expensive enough that a fast learned model is genuinely worth having) -- jax-cfd and TORAX are both already cheap and vmap-batchable, so building a fast surrogate over either has the least payoff of any domain here. Correct call, walking that filter back rather than defending it. Second, separate ask: something more intuitive to a lay audience than a stellarator or an airfoil. Landed on **insulated-mug/thermos thermal design** -- picked over two other candidates discussed (bridge/truss FEM, propeller BEMT) specifically because the physics is self-written (zero license question, unlike every wrapped-solver domain here) and cheap enough to spike end-to-end in one pass.

**Physics**: transient heat conduction through a mug/thermos wall, not a wrapped external simulator -- `scripts/thermal_mug_spike.py` implements a 1D (thin-wall/planar approximation, since wall thickness is small relative to a ~3.5cm mug radius) finite-volume conduction model, implicit (backward Euler, unconditionally stable, solved via `scipy.linalg.solve_banded` every step), liquid as a lumped-capacitance node, ambient as a fixed-temperature boundary. `x` = `[t_wall_mm, t_gap_mm, k_gap]` -- structural (steel) shell thickness, insulation-layer thickness, and insulation conductivity as a continuous stand-in for material choice (vacuum-like ~0.005 .. foam/air ~0.03 .. poor ~0.05 W/(m K), real order-of-magnitude values). `y` = `retention_time_s` (time to cool from 90C to 60C, `None`/flagged if not reached rather than silently clipped -- same convention as every other domain's non-convergence signal), `mass_kg` (material cost/weight proxy), `touch_temp_60s_C` (outer-wall safety temperature 60s after filling).

**Verified the FD implementation against a closed-form limit before trusting it** (same bar as §21's NACA0012 check, §45's analytic fluid_frac check): with wall/gap thermal mass scaled to ~0, the FD solution should collapse to a simple lumped-RC exponential decay with a closed-form time constant. Confirmed to **0.01-0.03% relative error** across two parameter sets -- the numerical scheme itself is correct, independent of how well the underlying physical model matches a real mug.

**A real discretization bug caught by a sanity check, not by inspection** (same pattern as §45's `grid.mesh()` axis swap, §33's sanity-filter gap): first pass at 15 fixed nodes per layer gave a *non-monotonic* `touch_temp_60s_C` at large `t_gap` (41.87C at 10mm, ticking back up to 43.44C at 20mm) -- physically wrong, since more insulation should only ever slow the outer surface's early-time warming further. Traced via a direct resolution sweep (N_GAP=15/40/100 -> 43.4/40.8/39.8C at t_gap=20mm, monotonically converging) to fixed node count leaving thick insulation layers under-resolved for the 60-second-early transient. Fixed by raising `N_GAP` 15->40 (cost: 37.8ms -> 40.3ms/candidate, negligible) -- monotonicity restored (40.50C at 10mm vs. 40.75C at 20mm, the residual near-flatness at large `t_gap` reads as a real physical plateau, not a bug: past a certain thickness the 60s outer-surface reading is governed by local near-surface diffusion into what's effectively a semi-infinite slab, not total thickness).

**Sanity checks pass, both sweeps monotonic in the physically correct direction**: (1) fixed material, sweeping insulation thickness 0/2/5/10mm -- retention 74.6/122.8/195.2/316.4 min, touch-temp 87.4/61.4/46.7/40.5C, both moving the right way, a large (>4x) and clearly-outside-discretization-noise effect; (2) fixed thickness, sweeping insulation quality (k_gap 0.05->0.005, poor->vacuum-like) -- retention 147/214/316 min -> not reached in 6h, touch-temp 54.9->44.5->37.5->29.8C, same direction, same magnitude of effect.

**Real, useful multi-objective structure found, not assumed** -- ran 300 random designs and checked correlations directly rather than reasoning from the sweeps alone: `corr(retention_time, touch_temp_60s)` = -0.876 (strong but not ±1 -- genuinely correlated as physics predicts, but with real independent variance, ~23% unexplained by a simple linear relationship between the two), while `corr(mass_kg, retention_time)` = 0.022 and `corr(mass_kg, touch_temp_60s)` = 0.052 -- both essentially zero. That last pair is the interesting finding: **mass is nearly orthogonal to performance across the random sample**, because `k_gap` (material choice, free -- doesn't add mass) drives retention/safety by an order of magnitude on its own (sweep 2), independent of `t_gap`/`t_wall` (which drive mass but only moderately affect performance on their own, sweep 1). This is a real two-axis design tension in the spirit of airfoil's `cl`/`cd`/`cm` or VMEC's `aspect_ratio`/`elongation`/`qi`: a good design isn't simply "add more material," it's a genuine combination of *how much* and *what* insulation -- exactly the kind of non-trivial manifold a generative-architecture comparison needs to be measuring something real (§18-20's earlier point about degenerate design spaces applies in reverse here: this one isn't degenerate).

**Speed**: ~40ms/candidate on host CPU, single-threaded, no batching attempted yet -- already cheaper than TORAX's 0.36s/candidate and VMEC++'s multi-second calls, in the same rough range as XFOIL. Trivially parallelizable across candidates (independent, no shared state) the same way `oracle_harness.py`'s subprocess batching already handles CPU-only domains.

**Current status, spike-only** (same stage every domain here started at): a verified, sanity-checked `x -> oracle -> y` shape exists with 3 real, non-redundant-enough-to-be-interesting targets, but no `DomainSpec`/`gym_schema.py` entry, no oracle module (`thermal_mug_spike.py` is a standalone spike script, not yet split into the project's `oracle_base.py`-style interface), no Dockerfile (arguably doesn't need one -- pure numpy/scipy, no GPU, no exotic pin, could run directly in `preprocess` or even the host the way `screen_*.py` scripts do), and no dataset/generative models.

**Open**:
- Design vector is thin (3 continuous scalars) -- same concern raised about jax-cfd's 1-2 Fourier coefficients applies here in miniature; worth deciding whether to add more design freedom (e.g. a second insulation layer, a non-uniform thickness profile, or an explicit discrete material category with realistic k/rho/cp triples instead of a free-floating `k_gap`) before training generators, or whether 3 dims is an intentional, honestly-scoped "simple domain" contrast to VMEC's 90+.
- Real-world calibration not attempted: `H_AIR=10`, `H_LIQ=300` W/(m2 K) are order-of-magnitude textbook values, not fit to any real mug; radiative losses are ignored entirely (a real cup radiates as well as convects, would speed real cooling further); no top/bottom (lid, base) heat loss modeled, only the cylindrical side wall. The bare-steel-mug case (74.6 min to drop 30C) is the same order of magnitude as everyday experience but not independently verified against a real measurement the way §36's real reference devices were.
- No validity signal beyond the retention-time `None` flag and an implicit finite-value check -- no analogue yet of VMEC/airfoil's oracle-failure classification.
- No dataset generation, `DomainSpec`, oracle module split, or generative-model training attempted -- this section is the feasibility spike only, matching TORAX's own post-§25 stage.

## 47. Mug domain productionized: oracle module, DomainSpec, and a real 20K-row dataset -- and a real censoring bug caught by the hit-rate check before it silently biased the data

Direct continuation of §46, closing its "spike only" gap the same way §31 built on top of TORAX's §25 spike. Built `mug_oracle.py` (`oracle_base.py` contract, wrapping `thermal_mug_spike.simulate()` unchanged -- no new physics), `mug_spec()` in `gym_schema.py` (4th real `DomainSpec` after VMEC++/airfoil/TORAX), and `generate_mug_dataset.py`.

**A real methodology bug caught by the hit-rate check itself, not by inspection** (same pattern as §33's sanity-filter gap, §45's axis swap): a first 20-candidate smoke test using §46's original `retention_time_s` target (time to cool to 60C) gave only a **20% hit rate** -- and every single rejection was "still above 60C" at the 6-hour simulated cutoff, not an actual instability. That's a silent bias, not a failure signal: it was throwing away the BEST designs (the ones insulating well enough to still be hot at 6h), the opposite of every other domain's non-convergence rejection (which discards broken candidates, not good ones). Root cause: a threshold-crossing TIME is open-ended and needs an arbitrary cutoff; a genuinely excellent thermos can legitimately take many hours to cross 60C (that's the whole point of a vacuum flask), so "not reached in 6h" conflates "broken" with "too good to measure inside this window."

**Fixed by changing the primary target's definition, not by extending the cutoff further** (that would only push the same problem out, not remove it): switched to **liquid temperature at a fixed 2-hour mark** (`temp_at_2h_C`) -- `simulate()`'s own `final_liq_temp_C` at `t_max=7200` already computes exactly this, always well-defined for any stable input. Re-ran the smoke test: **40/40 (100%) valid.** Confirmed at real dataset scale too -- **20,000 rows generated in 41s (100.0% hit rate)**, 28 workers, host CPU, no Docker. This domain's validity profile is now genuinely different from every wrapped-solver domain here: `ok=False` can basically only mean a structurally invalid input (non-positive thickness/conductivity), not a physics failure -- there's no non-convergence mode to have, since backward Euler is unconditionally stable. Worth flagging plainly as a real contrast, not assumed away.

**Correlation structure on the real 20K-row dataset, computed directly rather than trusted from the 300-sample spike**: `corr(mass_kg, temp_at_2h_C)=0.085`, `corr(mass_kg, touch_temp_60s_C)=-0.010` -- confirms §46's "mass nearly orthogonal to performance" finding at 65x the sample size. But a deeper look at the per-parameter correlations **refines that story rather than just repeating it**: `corr(t_wall_mm, mass_kg)=1.000` (essentially exact) while `corr(t_wall_mm, temp_at_2h_C)=0.067` and `corr(t_wall_mm, touch_temp_60s_C)=0.001` (both negligible) -- steel's density (8000 kg/m3) is ~200x the insulation's (40 kg/m3), so **`mass_kg` is almost entirely driven by the structural shell thickness, which is itself almost thermally irrelevant** (steel conducts so well its own thickness barely adds resistance next to the insulation layer). The REAL thermal tension lives entirely in the other pair: `corr(t_gap_mm, temp_at_2h_C)=0.690`, `corr(k_gap, temp_at_2h_C)=-0.544`, both with matching-sign effects on `touch_temp_60s_C` (-0.432, +0.584). **Read together: this domain has two nearly-separable axes, not one three-way tangle** -- a manufacturing/durability choice (`t_wall_mm`, drives cost, thermally near-irrelevant) and a thermal-design choice (`t_gap_mm`/`k_gap`, drives performance, doesn't move cost much). That's arguably a more realistic and more interesting structure than "more insulation costs more" would have been -- it means a designer can pick wall thickness for durability/dishwasher-safety reasons for free, and separately optimize insulation for thermal performance, matching how real product design actually decouples those two decisions. `temp_at_2h_C`/`touch_temp_60s_C` correlate at -0.930 (strong but not degenerate, ~14% independent variance -- the two targets aren't redundant, consistent with §46's transient-lag reasoning for why touch-safety was worth measuring separately).

**Current status**: real oracle module + `DomainSpec` + 20K-row dataset exist and are sanity-checked; still missing (same gap every domain has at this stage, per §29's readiness bar): no scoring model (bar C), no generative models (bar D), no `sanity_filter` (bar B partial -- all 20K rows came back finite/valid, but this hasn't been stress-tested against deliberately adversarial inputs the way §21 did for airfoils), and the design-vector-richness question from §46 (3 scalars, and now known to really be 2 effective thermal DOF plus 1 near-independent cost DOF) is still open.

**Open**:
- Whether to treat `t_wall_mm` as a genuinely separate, near-orthogonal design axis going forward (e.g. fixing it and reporting results purely over `(t_gap_mm, k_gap)`) or keep it as free-floating "extra realism" -- current data suggests it mostly adds cost-axis variance without a matching performance signal, which could make some steerability metrics look artificially strong or weak depending on how it's handled.
- No stress-test of adversarial/extreme inputs (e.g. `t_gap_mm` near the sampled max combined with the lowest `k_gap` -- did any of those land outside the sampled dataset ranges, and are they still numerically well-behaved?).
- No scoring model, cVAE/diffusion/GAN, steerability eval, or viewer built yet for this domain.
- `LOG_TARGET_NAMES` still unexamined -- `temp_at_2h_C`/`touch_temp_60s_C` both look roughly linear-range in the dataset stats above, `mass_kg` likely does too (dominated by a uniformly-sampled `t_wall_mm`), so probably fine to leave empty, not rigorously checked the way VMEC/airfoil's log-targets were originally identified.

## 48. Mug domain, v2: richer parameterization (named materials, a wall profile, a real handle) per direct user request -- three near-separable design axes found, not assumed

User pushed back on §47's 3-scalar design vector directly: wanted named materials (not one free-floating conductivity), a wall PROFILE (not a constant thickness), and explicitly asked for two new concerns -- weight ("wrist strain") and a handle that "shouldn't make the handle hot." All three are genuine new physics, not cosmetic relabeling -- built as `simulate_v2()` in `thermal_mug_spike.py`, v1's `simulate()` left untouched for reference.

**Materials**: three palettes (`STRUCTURAL_MATERIALS`, `INSULATION_MATERIALS`, `HANDLE_MATERIALS`), each a short list of real named substances (plastic/glass/ceramic/steel; vacuum/aerogel/air_gap/foam; silicone_rubber/wood/plastic_grip/ceramic/steel) with real order-of-magnitude (k, rho, cp) triples, sorted by conductivity. Each material "slot" in the design vector is a continuous index that linearly interpolates (k, rho, cp) between adjacent table entries -- keeps the parameterization fully continuous (generative-model-friendly, same style as v1's free-floating `k_gap`) while every point traces through real, nameable materials rather than an arbitrary number.

**Wall profile**: kept to 2 axial bands (rim, base) rather than a full height-resolved field -- real mugs often ARE thinner at the rim (comfortable drinking edge) and thicker at the base (stability/drop resistance), and 2 bands captures that without a 2D solve. Each band is its own independent 1D radial+gap conduction chain, splitting the cylindrical side-wall area evenly, both drawing from the same liquid.

**A real coupling-scheme decision, checked before trusting it**: v1 solved liquid+wall as ONE fully-implicit system (liquid as an unknown in the same tridiagonal solve). With 3 independent chains now needing to touch the liquid/body (rim, base, handle), that would require a non-tridiagonal star-shaped matrix. Used explicit-implicit splitting instead -- the liquid's own energy balance updates EXPLICITLY each step from the previous step's band fluxes, each chain (rim/base/handle) solves fully implicitly using the previous step's liquid/body temperature as a fixed boundary. **Verified this is still consistent with v1's own already-verified scheme, not assumed**: reduced to v1's exact case (equal bands, steel/foam) -- `final_liq_temp_C` 69.65C (v1) vs. 69.97C (v2, 0.46% relative), `touch_temp_60s_C` 46.67C vs. 46.68C. Close enough to trust the new scheme without re-deriving its own closed-form check.

**Handle: a genuine transient fin-conduction model, a quasi-steady shortcut explicitly checked and rejected first.** A quasi-steady approach (steady-state fin formula at each instant) implicitly assumes the handle's own thermal diffusion time is short vs. the 60s reporting window -- checked directly: `tau ~ L^2/alpha` for a ~5cm steel handle is ~667s, and for a low-conductivity handle (precisely the "safe" designs this target exists to identify) the ratio is no better -- neither clears 60s, so the transient had to be solved for real, not shortcut. Implemented as a 1D fin: implicit backward-Euler chain along the handle's length WITH a lateral convective-loss term at every cell (the standard extended-surface/fin equation term v1's wall chains never needed, since those only lose heat at their two ends), Dirichlet-coupled at the base to the previous step's average of the two wall bands' outer surface temperatures, adiabatic (zero-flux) tip (standard for a fin whose tip area is small vs. its lateral area). Starts at ambient, not fill temperature, unlike the wall -- physically correct, since the handle is remote from the liquid and only heats via conduction from the body.

**A real, non-obvious physical finding caught by a monotonicity sweep, not assumed away**: sweeping `HANDLE_MATERIALS` by conductivity at fixed length/diameter, `wood` (k=0.17) came out HOTTER than `plastic_grip` (k=0.20) at 60s -- backwards from "higher k = hotter" intuition. Traced to thermal diffusivity, not conductivity alone, governing short-time transient response: `alpha = k/(rho*cp)` -- wood's alpha (1.67e-7 m2/s) is actually higher than plastic's (1.108e-7) because wood's volumetric heat capacity (`rho*cp`) is lower, so despite lower k, wood's thermal front advances faster in the first 60 seconds. Real physics, not a bug -- confirmed by direct calculation, not dismissed -- and a genuine reason this domain's material index isn't simply "monotonic in k" for early-transient targets, worth remembering before assuming a generative model's material-steering behavior "should" be monotonic either.

**A second real finding from the same sweep, also checked rather than assumed**: at realistic handle lengths (>=~20mm) and 60s, every non-steel material sits at essentially ambient temperature (only `steel`, by far the highest-diffusivity material in the table, shows meaningful warming) -- checked out to 600s (10 min) too, same pattern holds. This is a genuine, physically correct result (it's *why* rubber/wood/plastic grips exist -- they really don't transmit body heat that far in that little time), not a data-generation flaw, but it does mean `handle_temp_60s_C` is heavily floor-dominated across the sampled space -- confirmed at real dataset scale below, not just the sweep.

**Real dataset regenerated for the new 8-dim design vector** (`generate_mug_dataset.py` updated for direct range sampling over all 8 params -- still no archetypal seeds needed, confirmed again by a 40/40 smoke test): **20,000 rows, 100% hit rate, 344s** (v2's ~227ms/candidate, ~5x v1's ~40ms given 3 coupled chains instead of 1, but still cheap and 100%-converging by construction). `handle_temp_60s_C`: **76.0% of rows sit within 0.1C of ambient** -- the floor-dominated shape predicted by the sweep, confirmed at scale, not just anecdote.

**The real payoff -- three largely separable design axes, checked via correlation, not assumed from the sweeps alone:**

| driver | temp_at_2h_C | mass_kg | touch_temp_60s_C | handle_temp_60s_C |
|---|--:|--:|--:|--:|
| t_gap_mm | 0.789 | 0.068 | -0.537 | -0.009 |
| insulation_material_idx | -0.347 | -0.037 | 0.214 | 0.012 |
| t_wall_rim/base_mm | 0.03/0.02 | 0.356/0.349 | ~0 | ~0 |
| struct_material_idx | 0.019 | **0.684** | 0.016 | -0.004 |
| handle_length_mm | -0.005 | 0.034 | -0.000 | **-0.371** |
| handle_material_idx | 0.015 | 0.074 | -0.011 | **0.385** |

Reads cleanly as three near-independent knobs: **(1) thermal performance** (`t_gap_mm`/`insulation_material_idx` drive `temp_at_2h_C`/`touch_temp_60s_C`, nothing else does), **(2) structural cost/weight** (`t_wall_rim/base_mm`/`struct_material_idx` drive `mass_kg` almost exclusively -- `struct_material_idx` alone at 0.684 is the single strongest correlation in the whole table, steel vs. plastic's ~8x density difference dominating), **(3) handle safety** (`handle_length_mm`/`handle_material_idx` drive `handle_temp_60s_C` and nothing else, both roughly balanced in magnitude and correctly signed). Cross-target correlations confirm the same story from the other direction: `mass_kg` vs. every safety/performance target is ~0 (0.007-0.107), `handle_temp_60s_C` vs. everything except its own two drivers is ~0 (-0.031 to 0.062). This is a materially richer structure than §47's two-axis finding -- a real design here trades off across three genuinely different concerns, matching the "GAN/VAE numbers would mean something real" bar this whole investigation started from, now with more to be selective about (a domain where selectivity -- does nudging one target avoid dragging the others -- has three, not two, largely-independent things to not drag).

**Open**:
- `handle_temp_60s_C`'s floor-heavy distribution (76% at-ambient) may need a transformed representation (e.g. log or a shifted scale) before training a scoring/generative model -- not yet tested whether raw-scale regression/steering handles this cleanly or needs the same care VMEC/airfoil's `LOG_TARGET_NAMES` gave their own skewed targets.
- No adversarial stress-test of extremes yet (e.g. shortest+fattest+highest-diffusivity handle, or thinnest+worst-material wall combined with best insulation) -- all 20,000 sampled rows were finite/valid, but that's uniform random sampling, not a deliberate edge-case sweep.
- No scoring model, cVAE/diffusion/GAN, steerability eval, or viewer built yet -- still bar A+B only on §29's readiness scale.
- v1's `simulate()`/`mug_oracle.py` history (§46-47) is now superseded for any future work on this domain, but left in the file for the regression check's own provenance -- not deleted.

## 49. Mug domain closes bars C+D: scoring model, all 3 generative architectures, and a first steerability battery -- the GAN>diffusion>cVAE ranking replicates in a 4th domain

Direct continuation of §48 per explicit user request to keep going. Closes §29's readiness bar for this domain (A/B done since §47-48; this section does C and D).

**Scoring model** (`train_mug_scoring.py`, plain MLP, same deliberately-simple starting point as VMEC's §1/airfoil's §29): trained on the real 20,000-row dataset, 80/10/10 random split. Compared against a computed naive-mean baseline (important here specifically because `handle_temp_60s_C` is 76% floor-dominated, §48 -- a model could look good on raw RMSE by mostly predicting the floor, so the honest comparison is against a baseline that ALSO always predicts the same thing). Real result: **90-97% error reduction across all 4 targets**, including `handle_temp_60s_C` (90.7%) -- confirms the model genuinely learned the floor/non-floor structure, not just parroting the mean.

**Generative models**: `train_mug_cvae.py`/`train_mug_diffusion.py`/`train_mug_gan.py`, all reusing `train_cvae.py`/`train_diffusion.py`/`train_gan.py`'s model classes unchanged with `n_nfp=0` (no aux conditioning, same situation as TORAX -- confirmed again here, not assumed, since mug_oracle.py's whole design vector is already the 8 continuous params). Trained cleanly, no schedule/seeding issues (both already fixed project-wide by §19/§28).

**Wired into `steerability_generic.py`** (added `mug_spec` to `SPEC_FACTORIES`, a `mug` entry to `gym_schema.get_conditioning` mirroring TORAX's `extra_dim=0` case exactly) -- smoke-tested at small scale first (2 anchors) before the real run, same discipline every prior domain's first wiring got.

**First real battery, 12 anchors, step_std=1.0, axis mode, targeted vs. null, seed=0:**

| model | validity | correct-dir (targeted) | correct-dir (null) | signal above chance | steer-convergence (targeted/null) |
|---|--:|--:|--:|--:|--:|
| cVAE | 90.8% | 78.9% | 59.1% | +19.8pp | 79.2% / 95.1% |
| Diffusion | 90.0% | 86.4% | 64.4% | +22.0pp | 76.4% / 91.7% |
| GAN | 90.8% | 91.7% | 65.4% | **+26.3pp** | 75.7% / 94.4% |

**The GAN>diffusion>cVAE ranking on signal-above-chance replicates for a 4th time** (after VMEC++ §18-20, airfoil §23, TORAX §37) under a genuinely different oracle (self-written FD conduction, not a wrapped solver), a genuinely different design vector shape (8 continuous params including 3 material-interpolation indices, no discrete aux), and a genuinely different target structure (3 near-separable axes, §48) -- strongest cross-domain confirmation yet that this ranking is architectural, not domain-specific. Steer-candidate convergence is roughly comparable across all 3 models here (75.7-79.2% targeted), unlike TORAX's own extreme diffusion-convergence-collapse (§37-38, ~48%) -- a real, useful negative result: whatever made TORAX's diffusion model uniquely bad at staying in-bounds when pushed (§38's finding: catastrophic extrapolation into physically-impossible geometry) is NOT a universal diffusion weakness, since it doesn't reproduce here or in VMEC/airfoil.

**Open**:
- Only axis-mode, single seed, single step_std -- no arbitrary-direction test, no magnitude sweep, no second seed yet (every other domain got this fuller treatment eventually, §19-20/§30).
- No coverage/novelty-diversity audit (§26/§33-34's own battery) run for this domain.
- No viewer built for this domain (§39-43's pattern not yet extended here).
- `handle_temp_60s_C`'s floor-heavy distribution's effect on steering specifically (does "correct direction" mean much when 76% of the space sits at one value?) not separately examined -- the aggregate correct-direction numbers above average across all 4 targets, not broken out per-target the way airfoil's cl/cd/cm/l_over_d split was in §22.

## 50. Mug domain, v3: user asked to "uplevel" it further -- picked a lid/top-loss model and a variable body shape from a 4-option menu, both real physics gaps, not cosmetic additions

Direct user request, offered 4 candidate directions with an honest ranking (lid/top-loss flagged as highest-value since v1/v2 had a real correctness gap, not just a missing nice-to-have -- the top of the mug lost ZERO heat, backwards from reality where an open liquid surface is typically a FASTER loss pathway than an insulated side wall). User picked lid/top-loss + variable body shape, deferred richer-handle and hot/cold-dual-mode. Built `simulate_v3()` in `thermal_mug_spike.py` (v2's `simulate_v2()` untouched for reference).

**Body shape**: radius at 3 heights (base/mid/rim) instead of one fixed cylinder radius -- taper, flare, and belly shapes are all expressible now (straight cylinder is just the r_base=r_mid=r_rim special case). Each half-height band is now a real frustum (truncated cone), not a plain cylinder shell -- lateral area (`frustum_lateral_area`) and liquid volume (`frustum_volume`) both computed from the actual geometry per candidate, not the fixed module-level constants v1/v2 used. Height stays fixed (not varied this pass -- flagged as a further idea, not done, to keep this pass's parameter growth bounded).

**Lid**: one continuous `lid_coverage_frac` in [0,1] interpolates open-cup (0) to fully-sealed (1) -- avoids a discrete branch, matching this domain's continuous-parameterization style everywhere else. The open fraction of the top loses heat via a temperature-DEPENDENT effective coefficient (`H_AIR_TOP_OPEN=15` baseline + `EVAP_COEFF=60` scaled by how far above ambient the liquid still is) -- an explicit, documented stand-in for evaporation (which really does scale with vapor pressure, which really does rise with temperature) rather than a full Antoine-equation model; chosen because the ballpark magnitude (open coffee cools much faster than lidded -- common experience) matters more here than the exact curve shape. The covered fraction conducts through a lid (own thickness + a material index reusing `STRUCTURAL_MATERIALS`, not a 4th materials table) to ambient at the plain convection coefficient.

**A real initial-condition bug caught by the coverage sweep itself, not by inspection** (same pattern as every prior domain bug here): first pass initialized the lid at fill temperature (`T0_LIQUID`), matching the wall bands' own convention. Result: `touch_temp_at_C` pinned near 90C at every coverage level >0 for the entire 60s window, regardless of lid thickness/material/coverage fraction -- a dead giveaway once compared against the coverage=0 case (46.55C, sane). Root cause: a lid isn't liquid-wetted at t=0 the way a submerged wall shell is (the wall-band convention is itself a stated fill-event approximation, §46) -- it only heats via conduction from below, so it should start at ambient, exactly like the handle already does. Fixed (`T_lid = np.full(N_LID, T_AMB)`) -- re-verified clean afterward.

**Verification, since a numeric regression match against v2 isn't the right bar here** (v2's assumption -- zero top loss -- is itself unphysical, not a baseline worth reproducing exactly): checked that v3 with a fully-sealed, thin realistic lid runs COOLER than v2's idealized zero-loss case (62.26C vs. 69.97C, correct direction -- a real lid still conducts some heat, v2 assumed none) while `touch_temp_at_C` stays close (46.44C vs. 46.68C, sensible since a thin lid barely warms in 60s). Coverage sweep (0/0.25/0.5/0.75/1.0): `temp_at_2h_C` rises monotonically (42.72->62.19C) -- more sealing, better retention, as expected; `touch_temp_at_C` jumps from 46.55C (no lid) to a roughly flat ~70.5-70.9C the instant any lid exists, independent of how MUCH area it covers -- confirmed this is real, not a bug: lid surface temperature is an intensive (per-unit-area) quantity, driven by material/thickness/liquid-temp only, genuinely independent of total covered area. Belly sweep (r_base=r_rim=35mm fixed, r_mid swept 25->60mm): liquid volume (0.285->0.725 L) and mass (0.211->0.327 kg) both rise as expected, and `temp_at_2h_C` also rises (44.82->59.26C) -- a bigger vessel at the same wall/insulation spec retains heat better per unit volume (surface-to-volume scaling, the same physical principle behind why larger animals retain body heat more efficiently), a real and previously-untestable prediction now confirmed. Speed: ~254ms/candidate (up from v2's ~215ms, the extra lid chain + top-loss term), still cheap.

**Real dataset regenerated for the 14-dim design vector** (`mug_oracle.py`/`generate_mug_dataset.py` updated; still no archetypal seeds needed, 40/40 smoke test): **20,000 rows, 100% hit rate, 412s.**

**The payoff -- correlation analysis shows the domain got MEANINGFULLY richer, not just bigger, and cross-target coupling actually DROPPED**, checked directly rather than assumed from the sweeps:

| driver | temp_at_2h_C | mass_kg | touch_temp_60s_C | handle_temp_60s_C |
|---|--:|--:|--:|--:|
| r_rim_mm | **-0.590** | 0.248 | -0.011 | -0.015 |
| lid_coverage_frac | **0.491** | 0.130 | -0.012 | -0.010 |
| r_mid_mm | 0.419 | 0.221 | 0.006 | -0.008 |
| t_gap_mm | 0.284 | 0.050 | -0.120 | -0.031 |
| t_lid_mm | -0.001 | 0.117 | **-0.904** | -0.003 |
| struct_material_idx | 0.042 | **0.598** | 0.011 | 0.000 |
| handle_length_mm | 0.017 | 0.032 | -0.002 | **-0.370** |
| handle_material_idx | -0.004 | 0.056 | 0.004 | **0.382** |

**`temp_at_2h_C` is now driven by FIVE interacting parameters** (rim radius/top exposure, lid coverage, belly volume, gap thickness, insulation material) instead of v2's two dominant ones (`t_gap_mm` alone was 0.789 in v2, now only 0.284 in v3) -- correctly reflecting that once an open-top loss pathway actually exists, side-wall insulation stops being the whole story, same as real life (an open mug's wall insulation barely matters if most heat leaves through the top). **`touch_temp_60s_C` is now almost entirely a LID story** (`t_lid_mm` at -0.904, by far the strongest single correlation in the whole table) -- completely decoupled from the wall/insulation parameters that drove it in v2 (all near zero here). **Cross-target correlation dropped sharply**: `temp_at_2h_C` vs. `touch_temp_60s_C` went from -0.836 (v2) to **-0.059** (v3) -- these were coupled in v2 because both were driven by the same `t_gap`/insulation combo; in v3 they're driven by almost entirely disjoint parameter sets (retention cares about rim/lid-coverage/body-volume/gap, safety cares about lid-thickness/material), so they can now be optimized nearly independently. **Body size now genuinely costs material** (`r_mid`/`r_rim` correlate 0.22-0.25 with `mass_kg`, impossible in v2 where geometry was fixed) -- a real, previously-unexpressable design fact (a bigger mug costs more) now captured. Handle axis (`handle_length_mm`/`handle_material_idx` -> `handle_temp_60s_C`) is untouched by any of this, confirming the new physics didn't disturb the existing clean axis.

**Reads as a genuine "uplevel"**: four now-largely-independent objectives (retention, body-touch-safety, handle-safety, cost) instead of v2's three, with retention itself now a real multi-parameter interaction rather than one dominant knob -- exactly the kind of richer manifold the original "will GAN/VAE numbers be meaningful" question (that started this whole domain) was asking for.

**Open**:
- Scoring model and all 3 generative-model checkpoints (§49) are now stale (`in_dim` mismatch, 8 vs. 14) -- not yet retrained against v3's dataset. Natural immediate next step, not done this pass.
- Height itself still fixed -- only radius varies; a further body-shape richness idea, not built.
- No stress-test of extreme geometry combos (e.g. r_base >> r_rim, an inverted-cone shape) for physical plausibility (negative-volume-adjacent edge cases) -- all 20,000 sampled rows were finite/valid, but that's uniform random sampling within already-sane ranges, not an adversarial sweep.
- `EVAP_COEFF`/`H_AIR_TOP_OPEN`'s exact values are order-of-magnitude engineering estimates, not fit to any real measurement -- flagged plainly in the code, not verified against real open-cup cooling data.

## 51. Mug viewer built -- 3D shape + live temperature-over-time animation, closing the one gap in the domain's otherwise-full parity with stellarators/airfoils/TORAX

Direct user request ("do we have good visualizations of this... 3D mugs and temperature changing over time"), confirmed first rather than assumed: no mug viewer existed at all (no export script, no physics service, no page) -- the one domain here without one. User chose full parity (UMAP/filter browser + live physics panel, matching every other domain) over a lighter demo-only scope.

**A real extension to `simulate_v3()` first**: it only returned two scalar snapshots (60s, 2h), no time-series -- added `record_series=True` (viewer-only, never used by the oracle/bulk-generation path), sampling liquid + each surface's outer temp every `series_dt=60` simulated seconds (~120 points over 2h, small enough to animate smoothly without a large payload) plus a `geometry` dict (real body/handle dimensions in meters) so the browser renders the actual frustum shape rather than reimplementing the geometry math in JS. Verified directly: 121 points, correct start (liquid/rim/base at fill temp, lid/handle at ambient -- matching §50's own fix) and correct end state.

**Infra**: added scipy/fastapi/uvicorn to the base `Dockerfile` (the one `preprocess` already uses) and a new `physics-mug` service (port 8093) -- this domain needed nothing exotic (pure numpy/scipy, the whole reason it was picked in §46/§48), so unlike airfoil/TORAX's physics services it reuses the plain base image rather than a dedicated Dockerfile. `mug_physics_service.py` mirrors `airfoil_physics_service.py`'s simplicity (synchronous, no JIT-warmup machinery, unlike TORAX's) -- ~250ms/call, no job-polling needed. `export_mug_viewer_data.py` follows the established `meta.json`/`X.bin`/`Y.bin` contract, single `bootstrap` source (no real-reference mugs exist yet, unlike airfoil's NACA shapes or TORAX's real devices -- flagged, not fabricated).

**A real bug caught immediately, before any browser testing**: `mug_physics_service.py`'s `/health` endpoint returned `dict[bool, bool]` (a typo -- should be `dict[str, bool]`), which FastAPI's response-model validation rejected outright, breaking the endpoint entirely. Caught by literally curling `/health` before touching the browser -- fixed in place, `uvicorn --reload` picked it up live.

**`mug.astro`**: adapted from `torax.astro`'s structure (styles, UMAP/filter-browser machinery reused near-verbatim -- none of it is domain-specific) with three real domain-specific pieces: (1) a 3D mug built from `THREE.LatheGeometry` over EXACTLY the 3 profile points the physics model itself uses (r_base/r_mid/r_rim) -- 2 straight-line segments, matching the model's real 2-band structure rather than a smoothed curve implying more shape detail than is simulated; a wedge left open (same ~297° convention as the TORAX viewer) reveals a liquid-core cylinder inside; a lid disc (area scaled by `lid_coverage_frac`, not exact placement -- flagged as a real, documented simplification) and a handle capsule, both added conditionally; (2) per-vertex height-based color interpolation (base-outer-temp at y=0 to rim-outer-temp at y=height) on a FIXED absolute color scale (20-90°C, ambient to fill temp) rather than TORAX's per-frame min/max -- chosen so colors stay directly comparable while scrubbing the time slider instead of re-normalizing every frame; (3) a single temperature-vs-time line chart (5 series: liquid, rim/base/lid outer, handle tip) replacing TORAX's 4-canvas radial-profile grid, with the same time-slider/play-button/current-position-marker mechanism.

**Verified live via claude-in-chrome, not just written** (this project's own standing bar): zero console errors across page load + multiple random-candidate clicks + slider scrubbing + metric-card expansion; confirmed real network calls (`POST /physics` -> 200) with visibly correct, differently-shaped 3D mugs per candidate (a straight narrow cylinder, a wide-lid tumbler, a bulging-belly shape all rendered distinctly and correctly from their own r_base/r_mid/r_rim); **scrubbing the time slider to t=0 visually reproduced §50's own initial-condition finding live** -- body renders hot (yellow, near fill temp, since it starts liquid-wetted) while the lid renders cool (dark blue, ambient) at the exact same instant, the bug that was caught and fixed in §50 now visibly confirmed correct in the browser, not just in a script's printed numbers.

**Open**:
- No real-reference mugs wired in (unlike airfoil's NACA shapes, TORAX's real devices) -- an open item inherited from every prior section, not newly introduced.
- Lid placement on the 3D shape is area-only (doesn't represent an off-center or partial-arc lid, which the model doesn't distinguish either) -- a real, stated simplification, not a bug.
- Handle is colored as one uniform tip-temperature value rather than a rendered gradient along its length (the physics DOES resolve a real gradient) -- a deliberate legibility tradeoff at this render scale, not a missing feature oversight.
- Play/pause button not exercised live in this pass (slider drag + candidate switching were) -- same caveat §40 flagged for TORAX's viewer at this stage.

## 52. Mug viewer, real presentation fixes -- direct user feedback that the wedge cutaway read as "a bite taken out of the mug," not a deliberate cross-section, plus the plasma color scheme reading as scientific/alien rather than "hot mug"

User's framing was precise and worth preserving: the TORAX-style wedge cutaway is a real, recognized convention for a tokamak's nested flux surfaces -- fine to keep there -- but a mug isn't usually understood that way, so the same wedge just looked like damage. Also asked for the top/bottom/opening to be unambiguous and for a color scheme that reads as "hot mug" rather than a scientific heatmap.

**Fixed with three changes to `mug.astro`'s 3D construction, all in `buildMugShape`/`updateMugColors`:**

1. **Full 360-degree body revolve** (`LatheGeometry(..., 0, Math.PI*2)`, dropping the `SHELL_PHI_SPAN` wedge entirely) -- the mug now reads as a solid, undamaged shape from any angle, opaque instead of the previous semi-transparent wedge-cutaway material (a real mug isn't see-through).
