## 95. Bracket viewer -- §93 still wasn't it either; the actual fix was rendering material presence/absence, not a colormap over a grid

Three rounds deep on this and each fix (§90, §92, §93) missed the mark by a different amount. The user's final, precise framing cut through it: *"a shaded grid is extremely unrelated to that problem. No mass is actually being removed... there's nothing physical about what you've actually done."* That's correct and it names the actual defect precisely -- every version so far, including §93's "generative search result" addition, colored EVERY cell by its density value (raw, contrast-stretched, or otherwise). A colormap never shows absence, because nothing in a colormap render is ever actually gone -- it's just a different shade. "Removing mass" has to look like removing something: real transparency where there's void, a real filled shape where there's material.

**Before landing on the fix, one real detour worth logging honestly**: the user's earlier question ("why is a rectangle related to a bracket at all?") led to a legitimate side-finding -- the domain IS the textbook Sigmund-99-line cantilever/MBB-beam problem (rectangular envelope, one clamped edge, one point load), not a domain shaped like an actual mounting bracket (which would typically fix at two bolt-hole regions, not one flat edge). Started building `scripts/bracket_simp_sweep.py` (a parallel 400-design SIMP sweep meant to replace the random-row browser with real optimized designs as the default view) as a response to that, but the user interrupted and clarified the actual complaint was rendering, not dataset composition or domain shape -- deleted the unused script rather than leave a dead, never-run artifact in the repo. The domain-shape gap (single-edge cantilever vs. a real two-mount-point bracket envelope) is real and separate from this section's fix; not addressed here, flagged as Open below.

**The actual fix, entirely in `bracket.astro`'s `drawRhoField`:**
- Threshold at rho=0.5 -- SIMP's own convention for "does this count as material" (`bracket_fea.py` was already treating this as the manufacturing-interpretation cutoff, just never used for rendering before).
- Void (rho < 0.5) is now drawn as **literal transparency** -- the dark page background shows through, so an empty region actually looks empty, not "darker."
- Material (rho ≥ 0.5) is drawn as an **opaque filled shape**, supersampled 8x per element (`sampleRhoBilinear`, a simple bilinear interpolation of the coarse 40x20 grid) and anti-aliased with a `smoothstep` band around the threshold, so the material/void boundary is a smooth edge rather than jagged single-pixel steps.
- A soft offset+blurred drop shadow is drawn beneath the solid shape -- a cheap, honest way to suggest physical thickness without fabricating an actual 3D extrusion (this stays a 2D plane-stress problem; a real 3D render would be overclaiming what's modeled).
- The 40x20 rectangle itself is now a **faint dashed outline** -- the fixed design envelope, visually and explicitly distinct from the material drawn inside it, directly answering "why is there a rectangle."
- `#densityRange` now also reports the solid fraction (e.g. "40% of cells count as material (rho ≥ 0.5)") alongside the raw min/max, so the SIMP volfrac constraint is directly checkable against what's rendered.

**Verified live in Chrome**: the SIMP `tip_load_vf0.4` reference now renders as a genuinely recognizable bracket-like truss -- solid gray members, real dark voids/holes, floating inside the dashed envelope, matching its 40% target volume fraction exactly (`40% of cells count as material` reported). The §93 generative-search-result design, run through the same renderer, came out as scattered disconnected gray islands, most not touching the fixed wall -- an honest and previously invisible confirmation of why that design is structurally weak (~7x worse compliance than SIMP): it isn't a coherent load path at all, which no amount of colormap coloring had ever made visible. A random dataset row (`bracket_reg_mlp`-training data, never optimized) rendered as a single solid rectangle with no voids at all when its raw range happened to sit entirely above 0.5 -- also correct and informative, not a bug: an unoptimized field has no reason to have carved anything out. No console errors. `docker compose run --rm pytest`: 14/14 passing.

**Open**: the domain-shape gap found along the way (single-edge cantilever vs. a real bolt-hole-constrained bracket envelope) is unaddressed -- would require redesigning `bracket_fea.py`'s boundary conditions (two or more fixed mounting regions instead of one clamped edge) and rerunning the full downstream pipeline (dataset/surrogate/VAE/search); not started, a genuinely separate and larger piece of work from this section's rendering fix.

---

## 96. Bracket domain -- fully removed, on direct user judgment that it never became physically sound

Direct instruction after §95 still didn't land: *"Nothing is even slightly valid... start deleting as much code as possible... you're not even remotely close to something physically sensible in any way shape or form."* Asked for the scope rather than guessing (full removal vs. keep-the-oracle vs. viewer-only) -- answer was full removal. Not treated as an overreaction to fix in place; four consecutive misses on the same complaint (§90, §92, §93, §95) is a real signal that the whole effort, not just its presentation, needs to stop rather than get a fifth patch.

**Removed, in full:**
- Scripts: `bracket_fea.py`, `bracket_oracle.py`, `generate_bracket_dataset.py`, `bracket_simp_baseline.py`, `train_bracket_scoring.py`, `train_bracket_vae.py`, `train_bracket_cvae.py`, `bracket_latent_optimize.py`, `export_bracket_viewer_data.py`.
- Viewer: `bracket.astro`, `glossary/bracket.astro`, the `viewer/public/data/bracket/` directory (root-owned by the container that wrote it -- removed via `docker compose run --entrypoint sh preprocess -c "rm -rf ..."` since the host user couldn't unlink it directly), the `bracket` entries in `DOMAIN_METRICS`/`METRIC_INFO` (`metricInfo.ts`), and every `/bracket` and `/glossary/bracket` nav link across `index.astro`/`airfoil.astro`/`torax.astro`/`mug.astro`/`mug-v2.astro`/the four other glossary pages.
- `gym_schema.py`'s `bracket_domain()` function, entirely -- the `CANDIDATE_TRIAGE` table's "Structural/Warp FEM" row reverted to its pre-build hypothesis shape (harness-fit genuinely unconfirmed again), with an honest note that this was attempted and reverted rather than pretending it was never tried.
- Generated artifacts: `output/bracket_*` (dataset, SIMP baseline, latent-search-best, stats/name JSONs), `checkpoints/bracket_*.pt` (VAE + 3 ensemble members), `logs/*bracket*.log`.

**Not removed: this and the preceding EXPERIMENT_LOG sections (§82-95)**. Deleting code that didn't work is right; deleting the record of what was tried and why it didn't work would just be hiding it, and the project's own logging convention (every prior domain's real mistakes, §s across the file) has never worked that way. The footer citation block below was updated to stop pointing at now-deleted files, but the narrative sections stay as the honest record.

**Verified after removal**: `grep -rli bracket` across `scripts/`/`viewer/src/` (excluding `node_modules`) returns only `gym_schema.py`'s own honest note about the reversion; `find -iname "*bracket*"` outside `.git` returns nothing. `docker compose run --rm pytest`: 14/14 passing (no test ever depended on the bracket domain). Loaded `/` live in Chrome after the removal: nav shows Stellarators/Airfoils/TORAX/Mug/Mug (v2) only, no dangling Brackets link, no console errors; confirmed `/bracket` now 404s via the viewer's own dev-server log rather than assumed.

**Also saved as a standing memory** (not just this log): a colormap/opacity render over a data grid cannot show "material removed," because nothing in it is ever actually absent -- check whether a render technique can represent absence before tuning color/contrast on it, and when a user says "confirm your understanding before proceeding," stop and answer in text before touching any tool.

**Open**: none for this domain -- it's gone. If a bracket/topology-optimization domain is revisited later, the two real, separate lessons from this attempt are (1) a topology-optimization problem needs boundary conditions that actually resemble the part being designed (a two-mount-point envelope, not a single-edge cantilever) before its output can look like what its name claims, and (2) a density-field render needs to represent absence as absence (real transparency, thresholded, not a color scale) from the first version, not the fourth.

## 97. Mug domain v2 -- a real, deep feasibility gap found by looking at the viewer: the handle's own ARC was never checked against the body, only its two endpoints

Direct user reaction to `/mug-v2` showing real accepted designs: *"None of these handle geometries are even vaguely approaching reasonable. None of them are connected correctly and one of them is just... stabbing randomly through the mug. This points at a very deep and fundamental bug."* Correct call, and glad it got checked rather than explained away -- this was NOT the same class of issue as §94's own axis-convention rendering bug (real, and fixed first, see below), it was a genuine gap in the ORACLE's own feasibility model that every prior gate missed.

**Two separate bugs found in this one investigation, fixed in order:**

1. **A real client-side rendering bug** (§94's own new code, not the oracle): `mug-v2.astro`'s body-mesh construction never converted from the Python FFD math's own `(x, y=r*sin(theta), z=height)` axis order into THREE.js's Y-up convention, while the handle's `pointAt` function already did that conversion correctly. Result: the body rendered with height along Z, the handle rendered with height along Y -- two meshes drawn 90 degrees rotated relative to each other in the same scene. Fixed in `gridToBufferGeometry`/`bottomCapGeometry` (a `emitPoint` helper doing the same swap `pointAt` already did), with a compensating triangle-winding reversal since a single-axis-pair swap is a mirror reflection that otherwise flips computed normals inside-out.

2. **The real, deep bug, still present after fix #1**: verified directly, not assumed -- with the rendering fixed, one candidate's handle STILL clearly cut through empty space inside the mug rather than looping around the outside. Traced numerically: sampled the handle's own Bezier centerline (`ffdgeo.bezier_path`, the same curve `mug_openfoam_oracle_v2.py`'s real simulate() already uses) and computed each point's distance to both the outer AND inner surfaces. The midpoint sat ~30mm from the outer surface AND ~25mm from the inner cavity surface SIMULTANEOUSLY -- only possible if that point is floating in open space inside the cavity, nowhere near either wall. Root cause: §77's own feasibility gate (`check_feasibility_fast`, folded into §85/§89's fast-filter tier) only ever checks that the handle's two ENDPOINTS sit within `ATTACH_TOLERANCE_M` of the outer surface -- it never checks the ARC IN BETWEEN. A handle whose two attach points sit on near-opposite sides of the body with an insufficient outward bulge satisfies that endpoint check perfectly while its real geometric path cuts straight across the interior -- exactly what the user saw. Every downstream gate (self-intersection, meshing) is blind to this too, since the handle and body are meshed as two INDEPENDENT solids connected only by the endpoint-distance constraint -- nothing anywhere validates what happens in the space between them.

**Fixed with one more real, cheap (no OpenFOAM subprocess) geometric gate in `check_feasibility_fast`**: bilinearly interpolate the outer surface's own LOCAL radius (distance from the central axis) at each centerline sample's real `(theta, z)` -- the same `(theta,z)`-grid interpolation convention the viewer's own (pre-existing, v1) body-coloring code already uses -- and reject if any INTERIOR centerline sample (excluding a margin near each real end, which legitimately sits at/inside the surface by construction, that's the attach disc) sits closer to the axis than the local outer surface. A genuinely outside-the-body loop passes; a chord cutting through the interior does not.

**Measured severity directly, not assumed to be rare**: re-ran the fixed `check_feasibility_fast` against the 5 candidates already sitting in the (pre-fix) `mug_v2_look` dataset -- **3 of 5 were actually invalid** under the corrected check. This means every dataset generated before this fix (`mug_v2_look`'s first two batches, and the `mug_v2_full` production run that had reached 87 accepted rows) is contaminated with a real, substantial fraction of physically-broken handle geometry that passed every prior gate. Stopped the running `mug_v2_full` container immediately, deleted all `mug_v2_full_*`/`mug_v2_look_*` output files (dataset rows AND the §91 worker-outcome classifier log, whose "accepted" labels are now stale relative to the corrected feasibility definition -- keeping it would have trained the classifier to prefer exactly the broken designs this fix rejects), and restarted both from zero.

`docker compose run --rm pytest`: 14/14 passing.

**Open**: the production run restarts from zero again (third time now, after §89's wall-thickness fix and §91's classifier-restart) -- each restart has been a real, judged trade of sunk compute against correctness, not free; worth checking whether the new gate's pass rate is stable before letting a long run go unattended again. The margin excluding "3 samples nearest each end" from the arc check is a reasonable-but-unvalidated choice, not derived from anything -- worth confirming it doesn't false-reject a legitimately tight, close-to-the-body loop if that turns out to matter once real data volume returns.

---

## 98. Bracket domain, rebuilt on Warp -- real 3D two-mount-point topology optimization, GPU-native, with a genuine 3D render this time

Direct rebuild instructions after §96's full removal: "don't build one [a solver]. Use warp" -- an explicit rejection of hand-writing another scipy-style solver, in favor of NVIDIA Warp's `warp.fem` (GPU kernel/autodiff FEM toolkit). Two further direct decisions before any code: 3D (not 2D) geometry, and two fixed mount points + a load point (not the single-clamped-edge cantilever that made §82-96 not read as a real bracket).

**Grounded the implementation in NVIDIA's own current source before writing anything**, not model memory of the API (fetched 2026-08-31 via WebFetch/WebSearch, not assumed): `warp/examples/fem/example_elastic_shape_optimization.py` (Hookean elasticity weak form, Dirichlet boundary projectors, wp.Tape autodiff pattern) and `example_diffusion_3d.py` (`fem.Grid3D` geometry constructor, `project_linear_system` + `bsr_cg` solve pattern). Both examples' code is real, current, and confirmed to exist in the installed `warp-lang` 1.17.0 package (including `warp.examples.fem.utils.bsr_cg`, checked directly rather than assumed included in the pip wheel).

**New `warp` Docker service** (`Dockerfile.warp`, GPU-enabled like `train`/`torax`) -- confirmed real GPU compute BEFORE writing any physics: a trivial kernel launched on `cuda:0`, result checked with `np.allclose`, both host GPUs (2x RTX 5000 Ada) visible to Warp.

**`scripts/bracket_fem_warp.py`** -- 3D linear-elasticity forward solve on a `Grid3D` domain (bounds (2.0, 1.0, 0.3), 40x20x6 resolution), two small fixed mount patches on the y=0 face (position-classified via `fem.normal`+`domain(s)`, not just face-direction like the source examples' left/right/bottom split), one load patch on the y=1 face. **Verified physically sensible before building anything on top**: mount-node displacement is EXACTLY 0.0 (hard Dirichlet, not just small), load-node mean displacement ~1.19 in the load's own applied direction, CG converged to 9e-9 residual.

**`scripts/bracket_simp_warp.py`** -- density-based (SIMP) topology optimization on top, using the standard self-adjoint compliance-sensitivity shortcut (compliance minimization under a fixed load needs no second/adjoint linear solve -- the adjoint variable equals -u itself; this is why the classic 99-line SIMP reference code never solves an adjoint system either, not a shortcut invented here). Two real bugs caught by watching the numbers do the wrong thing, not assumed correct:
- **Sign error**: differentiating `u^T K(rho) u` at fixed `u` gives `+u^T (dK/drho) u`, but the true compliance sensitivity is `dC/drho = -u^T (dK/drho) u` (standard adjoint derivation from `K u = f`). Missing the minus sign meant Adam was correctly minimizing the WRONG-signed quantity -- i.e., ascending true compliance. Caught because compliance was visibly getting worse each iteration (loss 7 -> 178 over 30 iters) while density collapsed, the exact signature of a flipped gradient.
- **Units bug**: `fem.integrate` returns raw integrals, not means (confirmed against the forward-solve script's own use of the same call to compute a boundary AREA, not "1") -- the volume-fraction penalty was comparing a raw volume integral directly against a 0-1 fraction target, silently aiming for the wrong volume fraction. Caught because the printed mean_rho at iteration 0 (0.18) didn't match the actual uniform initial density (0.3).

**After both fixes, a real, correct optimization**: compliance dropped 20x (6.45 -> 0.32) monotonically over 150 iterations, mean density held near the 0.3 target throughout (final 0.317), final density field genuinely near-binary (min 0.001, max 1.0, 34% of cells above the 0.5 material cutoff). Verified the RESULT is physically sensible, not just that the loss went down: mean density along the straight-line paths from each mount to the load point is 0.863 (solid), density everywhere else in the domain is 0.001 (void floor) -- material concentrated exactly where a real load-bearing truss should put it, and (checked explicitly) the result is exactly symmetric between the two mounts (0.8660/0.6436 identical to 4 decimal places for both), matching the symmetric problem setup.

**Real 3D render, not a colored grid -- direct response to "if you think there's a real 3d truss for me to look at then please let me see - I don't believe you"**: `scripts/bracket_export_mesh_warp.py` runs marching cubes (scikit-image, threshold 0.5) on the density field reshaped back into a regular grid (cell-center-to-grid-index recovered from the saved center coordinates directly, not assumed from Warp's internal dof ordering) -- 1,348 vertices, 2,692 faces, a real triangulated surface. New `viewer/src/pages/bracket.astro` (THREE.js + OrbitControls, same real-3D-reconstruction pattern already proven for `/mug-v2`) renders the actual mesh, both mount points (orange spheres) and the load direction (yellow arrow), with the design envelope as an explicit dashed wireframe box separate from the solid part -- carrying forward the one framing fix from §95 that was genuinely right (envelope != material) into a domain that's actually correct this time.

**Verified live in Chrome, not just that it renders something**: loaded `/bracket`, confirmed a real mesh renders (1,348 verts / 2,692 faces reported matching the export). Initial camera angle showed only one mount sphere -- checked whether that was a real asymmetry (queried the density data directly: both mounts' path/local density are identical to 4 decimal places, confirmed symmetric) before concluding it was pure camera occlusion, then rotated the view (dispatched real `PointerEvent`s on the canvas, same coordinate-drift workaround class as earlier sessions' `javascript_tool` clicks) to confirm both mount points and both legs of a genuine V-shaped truss are present and visible. Sent the resulting screenshot directly to the user for their own validation. No console errors. `docker compose run --rm pytest`: 14/14 passing.

**Open**: no `oracle_base.py`-contract wrapper, no dataset generation, no surrogate/VAE/latent-search layer yet -- this section is the verified physics + a real render only, deliberately not rebuilding the rest of the pipeline until the foundation itself was confirmed sound (given §82-96's history, confirming physics and rendering FIRST, separately, was the explicit point). Resolution is coarse (40x20x6, chosen for a fast first correctness check) -- worth increasing before this becomes the domain's real dataset-generation resolution. `gym_schema.py`'s `bracket_domain()` was not rebuilt.

---

## 99. Bracket domain -- parameterized the Warp SIMP pipeline and drove a genuinely different case through it end to end

Direct follow-up to §98's validated result: "let's go forward and see if we can actually optimize one ourselves." §98's scripts had mount/load positions as Python module-level globals referenced directly inside `@fem.integrand`-decorated kernels -- fine for the one correctness check, but Warp bakes global lookups into the compiled kernel at JIT time, so mutating them per-run isn't safe or defined. Refactored `bracket_fem_warp.py`/`bracket_simp_warp.py`/`bracket_export_mesh_warp.py` so mount positions, mount radius, load position/radius, the load vector, domain bounds, resolution, volume fraction, SIMP penalty, and iteration count are all real function/CLI arguments -- the mesh exporter now reads a run's own saved `{tag}_meta.json` instead of assuming the original case's constants, so it works for any `--out-tag`.

**Regression check before trusting the refactor**: reran the exact original case (`bracket_warp_simp`, all defaults) through the newly-parameterized code -- bit-for-bit identical result to §98 (compliance 0.317604, mean_rho 0.3169, 34.25% of cells above the material cutoff), confirming the refactor changed nothing about the actual computation.

**Then drove a genuinely different case**, not just a rerun: asymmetric mounts (x=0.25/1.5, off-center from the domain's midline), an off-center load position (x=1.3) with an ANGLED load vector (0.05, -0.14, 0.06 -- not purely vertical like §98), a leaner 25% volume budget (down from 30%), and a higher resolution (56x28x8 = 12,544 density cells, up from 4,800) for a visibly cleaner mesh. Real result, GPU wall-clock 28.4s total: compliance dropped 33.1x (56.622 -> 1.710) over 200 iterations, final volume fraction 0.305 (target 0.25 -- a bit of soft-penalty drift, same quadratic-penalty behavior as §98, not a new issue). Marching-cubes mesh: 4,607 vertices, 9,258 faces -- a visibly different, denser structure with a real curved void between its two legs, distinct from §98's simpler V-truss.

**Viewer**: `bracket.astro` now has a Case dropdown (`v1`: the original symmetric case, `v2`: this new asymmetric one) backed by two separate exported mesh JSONs (`mesh.json`/`mesh_v2.json`), and a real Solve Stats panel reads directly off each case's own saved metadata -- compliance start->end, improvement multiplier, target vs. actual volume fraction, and the actual load vector -- instead of static copy. The load-direction arrow now uses the case's real load vector (previously hardcoded to always point straight down, which would have been silently wrong for this angled case).

**Verified live in Chrome**: switched the case dropdown via a real `change` event (not assumed to work), confirmed the stats panel updated to the v2 case's own numbers (33.1x, 0.25/0.305, angled load vector) and the 3D view swapped to the new, visibly different mesh. Sent both cases' screenshots directly to the user. No console errors. `docker compose run --rm pytest`: 14/14 passing.

**Open**: same as §98 -- no oracle wrapper, dataset generation, or surrogate/VAE/latent-search layer yet. Both cases still only cover a single point-load-on-two-mounts topology; a real dataset generator would need to sample across this now-real parameter space (mount positions, load position/direction/magnitude, volume fraction) the way `generate_bracket_dataset.py` sampled DCT coefficients in the removed domain -- not started.

---

## 100. Bracket domain -- caught a real under-convergence bug by asking "is this actually optimal, or just the obvious answer?" and building a naive baseline to check

Direct, well-placed skepticism after §99's two cases: *"the v shaped trusses aren't actual optimal or anything are they? There's non-trivial design and optimization to be done?"* A fair question -- for a single point load connecting to two point supports, two straight struts is close to the textbook-obvious statics answer, so seeing a V-truss come out doesn't by itself prove the optimizer did anything non-trivial.

**Built a direct, honest test rather than asserting an answer**: `scripts/bracket_naive_baseline_warp.py` constructs a NAIVE density field by hand -- two straight cylindrical tubes directly connecting each mount to the load point, tube radius chosen via bisection on the discretized grid so its volume fraction matches the SIMP run's target exactly -- then runs it through the *same* forward solver (same SIMP stiffness interpolation, same boundary conditions) used to score the optimized result, so the comparison is apples-to-apples at matched material budget.

**First result was bad news, reported honestly rather than reframed**: the naive two-tube guess (compliance 0.277) actually BEAT the §99 "optimized" v1 result (0.318) -- despite the SIMP run using MORE material (31.7% vs. the naive baseline's exact 30%). A real problem, not a rounding difference.

**Diagnosed rather than assumed**: checked whether this was under-convergence by just running longer. At 600 iterations (up from the original 150), compliance was already down to 0.261 -- below the naive baseline. Kept extending: at 1500 iterations, compliance is flat to 5 decimal places (0.258600 at iter 1420, oscillating by <0.00004 through iter 1500) -- genuine convergence, not still descending. **Confirmed real, non-trivial optimization gain once actually converged**: v1's final compliance (0.2586) beats the naive two-tube baseline (0.2771) by ~7%; v2's (asymmetric mounts, angled off-center load) beats its own naive baseline by a much larger margin -- 2.37x (naive 3.251 vs. SIMP-converged 1.370) -- consistent with the intuition that a more asymmetric problem leaves more room for a genuinely non-obvious material layout to beat the "obvious" straight-line guess.

**Root cause and fix**: `bracket_simp_warp.py`'s default `--n-iters` was 150, copied from §98's original quick correctness check without ever separately confirming that was enough to actually converge for real optimization runs -- it wasn't, by a wide margin (150 iterations left compliance still measurably descending, and the "converged" answer was BEATEN by the naive guess it should have easily beaten). Fixed the default to 1500 (the actual checked convergence point for this problem size), with the naive-baseline numbers recorded directly in the script's own comment so the reasoning doesn't get lost. Re-ran both v1 and v2 cases with the corrected default, re-exported both meshes (v1: 1,338 vertices/2,672 faces, subtly more refined than the under-converged version; v2: 4,538 vertices/9,068 faces), and pushed both to the viewer -- verified live in Chrome, stats panel showing the corrected 24.9x (v1) improvement, no console errors. `docker compose run --rm pytest`: 14/14 passing.

**Open**: the naive-baseline check is currently a manual script run (`bracket_naive_baseline_warp.py --simp-compliance <value>`), not wired into `bracket_simp_warp.py`'s own output automatically -- worth adding as a standard printed comparison on every run, given how easily an under-converged result can look plausible (a real V-truss, a monotonic compliance curve, a volume fraction near target) while still being worse than a first-year-statics guess. The convergence-iteration count (1500) was checked for this specific problem SIZE (~5k-12k density cells) and Adam learning rate (0.02) -- not verified to generalize to substantially different resolutions or learning rates without rechecking.
